## Reasoning

**Champion analysis:**
The champion (score: 2924.22) is dramatically better than others. It uses:
- State 0: GoToColor (clr=3=blue walls), transitions: neighbors>3 → back to state 0 (self? No - D=0 with 2 states means the other state, which is state 1), or black floor → state 1 (with p=0.9)

Wait, let me re-read: with 2 states, leaving state 0, D=0 targets state 1. So:
- State 0 (GoToColor blue): if neighbors>3 → state 1; if black floor → state 1 (p=0.9)
- State 1 (FlockCenter fc=20): if gray floor → state 0 (p=0.25); if neighbors<6 → state 0 (p=1.5? No that's steepness); if random p=0.15 → state 0

So: robots go toward blue walls. When they hit black floor (target zone) OR have many neighbors, they switch to FlockCenter. When on gray floor they go back to seeking blue. This makes sense!

**Key insight:** clr=3 is blue - robots navigate toward the blue-walled strip. Black floor = target. The behavior is elegant: seek blue walls → reach black floor → flock center → if drift to gray → seek blue again.

**Exploitation strategy:**
- Vary fc1, probabilities, thresholds slightly
- Try adding more transitions to state 1 (e.g., detect blue walls)
- Try different neighbor thresholds

**Exploration:**
- 3-state versions: seek blue → flock → stop
- Try GoToColor blue + attraction
- Try different neighbor counts for transitions

## Reasoning

**Key observations:**
- The champion (2924.22) uses 2 states: GoToColor(blue) → FlockCenter, with black floor triggering the switch and gray floor/low neighbors/random triggering return.
- Several close variants also score well (2955-3232). The core structure is robust.
- The 3-state variant with Attraction as state 2 (2955.48) is interesting but slightly worse.
- Adding a random small-probability transition from state 0 (0.03) also works well (3232).
- The "stop" state variants and 4-state versions perform worse.
- Key parameters to tune: fc1 (15-25 range), p0x1 (black floor prob: 0.85-0.95), neighbor threshold/steepness, gray floor prob in state 1.

**Strategy:**
1. SURVIVAL: Keep champion exactly.
2. EXPLOITATION: Fine-tune the champion and top performers - vary fc1 (10-30), p0x1 (0.85-0.97), w0x0 (2.0-3.5), p1x0 (0.2-0.35), neighbor thresholds.
3. EXPLORATION: Try variations with:
   - fc1 values lower (10) or higher (30-40)
   - Different neighbor thresholds (2, 4, 5)
   - Adding a 4th transition to state 1 (look for blue walls to return to GoToColor)
   - Combining GoToColor with GoToColor in sequence (two different colors)

The champion's state 1 transitions back to state 0 on: gray floor (p=0.25), neighbors<6 (p=1.5 steepness), random (p=0.15). These seem well-calibrated. Let me try small variations.

## Reasoning

**Champion analysis (2784.39):**
3-state machine:
- State 0: GoToColor(blue, clr=3) → State 1 on black floor (p=0.9), or State 1 on neighbors>3 (w=2.5)
- State 1: FlockCenter(fc=20) → State 0 on gray floor (p=0.25), State 0 on neighbors<6 (w=1.5), State 0 random (p=0.15)
- State 2: Attraction(att=3) → State 0 on black floor (p=0.8), State 1 on gray floor (p=0.3)

Wait - state 2 is never entered from state 0 or 1 in the champion! Let me re-read. With 3 states, from state 0: D=0→state1, D=1→state2. From state 1: D=0→state0, D=1→state2(?). From state 2: D=0→state0, D=1→state1.

Actually D indexes into the OTHER states. With states 0,1,2:
- From state 0: D=0→state1, D=1→state2
- From state 1: D=0→state0, D=1→state2  (but all transitions use D=0, so only go to state0)
- From state 2: D=0→state0, D=1→state1

So state 2 is only reachable if state 0 or 1 transitions to it. In the champion, state 0 has n0x1 with D=1 → state 2 (when neighbors>3). State 1 transitions all go D=0 → state 0. State 2 goes back to state 0 (black floor) or state 1 (gray floor).

So the flow is: GoToColor → (many neighbors) → Attraction → (black floor) → GoToColor, OR GoToColor → (black floor) → FlockCenter → (gray/random) → GoToColor.

**Key insight:** The Attraction state handles the case where robots have many neighbors (clustering) to pull them tighter. This is the improvement over the 2-state version.

**Exploitation:**
- Vary att2 (2.5-4.0), p2x0 (0.75-0.9), fc1 (15-25)
- Try different neighbor thresholds for the state 0→state2 transition
- Try adding a 4th transition to state 1 that goes to state 2

**Exploration:**
- Try Attraction in state 1 instead of FlockCenter
- Try 4-state version with GoToColor→FlockCenter→Attraction→Stop
- Try varying the steepness w0x1

## Reasoning

**Final round analysis:**
- Champion (2713.42): fc1=19, w0x1=2.5 - hasn't improved in 3 rounds
- This round's best performers all scored worse than champion
- Key observations:
  - fc1=17 consistently scores ~2880-2970 (worse than champion's 19)
  - w0x1=2.6 is bad (3281!), w0x1=2.5 is best
  - att2=3.1 is worse than 3.0
  - The champion appears to be near a local optimum
  
**What hasn't been tried much:**
- fc1=19 with different w0x1 values closer to 2.5 (e.g., 2.45, 2.55)
- Different p0x0 values (only tried 0.88, 0.9, 0.92 - what about 0.91?)
- Different p1x0 (gray floor return prob): tried 0.22, 0.25, 0.27, 0.3 - 0.25 wins
- p1x2 (random escape): 0.13 was bad, 0.15 is good, 0.17 untested well
- w1x1: 1.3 bad, 1.5 good, 1.7 tried - 1.5 wins

**Duplicates in worst list:** The champion FSM appeared twice in worst! This suggests high variance - some runs of the exact champion scored badly. So the score has high stochasticity.

**Strategy:** Focus on very tight exploitation around the champion, and a couple genuine exploration attempts with different fc ranges or slight topology changes.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

At every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is outside the target region. The score does not depend on how close the robots are to each other.

The arena has two strips at opposite ends. The floor is BLACK in both strips and GRAY in between. The target region is the strip whose bordering walls are BLUE; the walls bordering the other strip are GREEN.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 14 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.34 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=6  Spiral        -> traces an expanding spiral
            requires --spd<I>  integer in [1..100]  (spiral speed)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=10  FlockCenter   -> moves toward the centre of the group
            requires --fc<I>  integer in [1..100]  (flocking gain)
  B=11  ZigZag        -> moves in a zigzag pattern
            requires --zfr<I>  integer in [1..100]  (zigzag frequency)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
